Curve locators recover unknown errors while fixing a protected value

16 October 2026 · Working proof of an established geometric recovery mechanism · Marked curve recovery. Produced by Codex (gpt-6-astra, medium reasoning).

Chosen. Develop the curve-locator lead on application and geometric-repair-application, before the coding-rank head, following the user's priority order. The missing implication is recovery from unknown error positions with an explicit positive-genus family and complete construction and pole budgets. A nonzero locator for a known divisor alone does not supply it.

Owed attempt. The lead asks for a concrete additional provider retaining its genus cost and distinguishing sections from rational functions. This entry constructs an observable linear system, proves that every accepted solution kills the actual error, and supplies an explicit Hermitian family. There is no previously deferred attempt on this item.

General statement. thm:marked-curve-recovery gives geometric certificates for the established two-system recovery mechanism, including a prescribed locator value. cor:hermitian-repair-family makes the curve, points, bases, input representation and costs explicit at unbounded parameters. These are an application and synthesis of standard algebraic-geometry decoding, not a new decoding algorithm or a better decoding radius.

Marked curve recovery

Statement. Let \(X/k\) be a smooth projective geometrically integral curve over a finite field, of genus \(g\). Let \(P_1,\ldots,P_n\) be distinct rational points and \(A,G\) divisors of nonnegative degrees \(a,m\), whose supports avoid those points. Write \(L(B)=H^0(X,\mathcal O(B))\) as rational functions with \((u)+B\ge0\), together with zero. Let \(t\ge0\), and assume

\[a\ge t+g,\qquad n>a+m+t.\tag{1}\]

Given \(y_i=f(P_i)+e_i\), with unknown \(f\in L(G)\) and at most \(t\) nonzero \(e_i\), solve the homogeneous linear system

\[\lambda\in L(A),\quad N\in L(A+G),\qquad N(P_i)=\lambda(P_i)y_i\quad(1\le i\le n).\tag{2}\]

There is a nonzero solution, every nonzero solution has \(\lambda\ne0\), and every such solution satisfies \(N=\lambda f\) as rational functions and \(\lambda(P_i)e_i=0\) at every coordinate. Recover \(f\) by solving the second linear system

\[h\in L(G),\qquad \lambda(P_i)h(P_i)=N(P_i)\quad(1\le i\le n).\tag{3}\]

This system has the unique solution \(h=f\). If a further rational point \(R\) avoids the evaluation points and the supports of \(A,G\), and \(a\ge t+2g\), one may instead impose \(\lambda(R)=1\) in (2). That affine system is consistent, and every solution has the same recovery properties and satisfies \(N(R)=f(R)\). The value \(f(R)\) is recovered without receiving it. With bases and evaluation matrices for \(L(A),L(G),L(A+G)\) supplied, both variants use \(O(n^3)\) field operations and \(O(n^2)\) field elements of memory. Basis and evaluation construction is a separate input cost until an explicit family is supplied below.

Proof: existence. Let \(E=\sum_{e_i\ne0}P_i\), of degree \(e\le t\). Riemann–Roch gives \(\ell(A-E)\ge a-e+1-g\ge1\); this is cor:curve-locator-budget, whose full proof and defining hypotheses were reread. A nonzero \(\lambda\in L(A-E)\) vanishes as a rational function at the erroneous coordinates, since the support of \(A\) avoids them. Taking \(N=\lambda f\in L(A+G)\) solves (2). In the marked case, the same recorded locator lemma gives \(\lambda(R)=1\), since \(a\ge e+2g\). Its proof uses \(H^1(A-E-R)=0\) and the evaluation exact sequence. The algorithm need not know \(E\); it solves (2) using only \(y\) and the supplied spaces.

Proof: every solution works. A nonzero \(u\in L(B)\), with \(\deg B=b\) and with the evaluation points outside its support, has at most \(b\) zeros among those points: its effective divisor \((u)+B\) has degree \(b\). If \(\lambda=0\) in (2), then \(N\) vanishes at all \(n>a+m\) points, hence \(N=0\). For any solution, \(N-\lambda f\in L(A+G)\) vanishes at all \(n-e\ge n-t>a+m\) correct positions. It is therefore zero identically. Substitution in (2) proves \(\lambda(P_i)e_i=0\). If \(h\) also solves (3), the function \(\lambda(h-f)\in L(A+G)\) vanishes at all \(n>a+m\) points, so is zero. The function field is a domain and \(\lambda\ne0\), hence \(h=f\). This is injectivity on the clean message space, even though multiplication by \(\lambda\) need not be invertible on all evaluation vectors. In the marked case, evaluating \(N=\lambda f\) at \(R\) gives the asserted protected value.

Proof: representation and cost. Choose bases of the three Riemann–Roch spaces. Equation (2) has \(n\) rows and \(\ell(A)+\ell(A+G)\) columns, plus one row in the marked variant; (3) has \(n\) rows and \(\ell(G)\) columns. For any divisor of nonnegative degree \(b\) on this curve, \(\ell(B)\le b+1\). One proof extends the field to an algebraic closure and evaluates at \(b+1\) distinct points away from the support: the zero bound makes evaluation injective, and scalar extension preserves dimension. By (1), \(a+m

What is repaired and what survives

Set \(U=\operatorname{ev}L(A)\), \(C=\operatorname{ev}L(G)\), \(H=\operatorname{ev}L(A+G)\) on the \(n\) received coordinates, and let \(Q:k^n\to k^n/H\) be the explicit quotient tester obtained by elimination. Then \(U*C\subseteq H\). For an error supported on at most \(t\) positions, \(H\) has no nonzero vector on those positions, because its minimum distance is at least \(n-a-m>t\). Thus \(Q\) detects every possible nonzero product \(\lambda*e\). Its error-action rank is at most \(t\), whereas evaluation on \(L(A)\) is injective and \(\dim U=\ell(A)\ge a+1-g>t\). Multiplication by every nonzero locator is injective on \(C\), by the same zero bound. These verify each hypothesis of thm:bilinear-error-recovery from divisor data, without assuming a successful locator.

Equation (2) is the explicit lift of \(Q(\lambda*y)=0\): \(N\) witnesses membership in \(H\). In the marked case use the evaluation algebra on \(P_1,\ldots,P_n,R\) to view \(\lambda\) as one multiplier annihilating the defect vector \((e_1,\ldots,e_n,0)\) while fixing the \(R\)-coordinate idempotent. It fixes the protected value and permits full message recovery. The extra coordinate is not supplied to the decoding algorithm; it is evaluated after (3).

The multiplicative budget is \(L(A)L(G)\subseteq L(A+G)\), with total pole-divisor degree \(a+m\); the observable detection margin is \(n-t>a+m\). On a one-point curve \(F_b=L(bP_\infty)\), multiplication obeys \(F_bF_c\subseteq F_{b+c}\). Consequently a fixed multiplier of pole order at most \(a\) carries a source expression of pole budget \(B\) through \(a+B\), using the same completed-product argument as certified repair composition. This is the geometric provider of the repair principle, with the bilinear theorem as its precise existing interface. It is not an identification of pole filtration with total polynomial degree or of this evaluation algebra with an unsatisfiable bounded-PC quotient. The arbitrary-error finite-grid contraction theorem is not invoked on the curve.

An explicit positive-genus family

Statement. For every \(s=2^r\ge4\), work in a supplied representation of \(k=\mathbb F_{s^2}\) and take the projective Hermitian curve

\[Y^sZ+YZ^s=X^{s+1},\qquad P_\infty=(0:1:0),\qquad R=(0:0:1).\]

Use all affine rational points except \(R\) as the received coordinates. Put

\[n=s^3-1,\quad g=s(s-1)/2,\quad m=s^3/4-1,\quad t=s^3/8-1,\quad a=t+2g,\quad G=mP_\infty,\quad A=aP_\infty.\tag{4}\]

The marked algorithm recovers every \(f\in L(mP_\infty)\) after arbitrary errors at at most \(t\) coordinates and produces a locator with \(\lambda(R)=1\). The message dimension is \(m-g+1>0\). Points, bases and evaluation matrices can be constructed in \(O(n^3)\) field operations with \(O(n^2)\) memory, including the subsequent two-system decoder. Thus no Riemann–Roch-space oracle is hidden for this family. Field construction itself is excluded by the supplied-field representation; these are field-operation, not bit-complexity, bounds.

Geometry and point count. The three homogeneous partial derivatives are respectively \(X^s,Z^s,Y^s\) up to nonzero signs, so they never vanish simultaneously at a projective point. The curve is smooth over the algebraic closure. A smooth plane curve is geometrically integral: distinct positive-degree components would meet by projective Bézout and create a singularity, and a repeated component would also be singular. Its degree is \(s+1\), so the plane-curve genus formula gives \(g=s(s-1)/2\). Both formulas are standard imported curve facts, also stated for this family in Høholdt–van Lint–Pellikaan, Example 2.74; their specific smoothness and count checks are supplied here.

The only point at infinity is \(P_\infty\). For each \(x\in\mathbb F_{s^2}\), \(x^{s+1}\) lies in \(\mathbb F_s\). The trace \(y\mapsto y^s+y\) is a nonzero \(\mathbb F_s\)-linear map onto \(\mathbb F_s\), with kernel of size \(s\): it is not the zero map since the polynomial \(Y^s+Y\) has at most \(s\) roots in a field of size \(s^2\). Thus there are exactly \(s\) affine \(y\)'s for each \(x\), hence \(s^3\) affine points. Remove \(R\) to obtain \(n\).

Explicit bases and filtration. In the chart \(Y=1\) at infinity put \(u=X/Y,v=Z/Y\). The equation is \(v+v^s=u^{s+1}\). Since its derivative with respect to \(v\) is one, \(u\) is a uniformizer; \(1+v^{s-1}\) is a unit and \(\operatorname{ord}_{P_\infty}v=s+1\). Therefore the affine coordinates \(x=X/Z=u/v\), \(y=Y/Z=1/v\) have pole orders \(s,s+1\). The affine coordinate ring is \(k[x,y]/(y^s+y-x^{s+1})\), free over \(k[x]\) on \(1,y,\ldots,y^{s-1}\), by monic division. A rational function with poles only at infinity is regular on this smooth affine complement, hence lies in that coordinate ring. The weights \(si+(s+1)j\), for \(i\ge0,0\le j

\[\mathcal B_b=\{x^iy^j:i\ge0,\ 0\le j

is a basis of \(L(bP_\infty)\) for every \(b\ge0\). The reduction \(y^s=x^{s+1}-y\) never increases pole weight, although it can increase ordinary total degree. This distinction is retained in every cost statement.

Budget and construction. The identity \(a=t+2g\) supplies marked locator existence. The detection margin is

\[n-(a+m+t)=s^3/2-s^2+s+2>0\qquad(s\ge4).\tag{6}\]

Also \(m>2g-2\), since \(m-(2g-2)=s^3/4-s^2+s+1>0\) for \(s\ge4\). Riemann–Roch therefore gives the stated message dimension. List field elements in the supplied representation and enumerate the at most \(s^4\) affine pairs, testing the equation by repeated powering in \(O(\log s)\) operations per pair. This costs \(O(s^4\log s)\subseteq O(n^3)\), and only the accepted points need storage. Enumerate the integer pairs in (5) for \(b=a,m,a+m\). Each list has at most \(b+1=O(n)\) elements. At each accepted point, precompute powers of \(x\) and \(y\) through the largest needed exponents, then form all basis evaluations; this takes \(O(n^2)\) field operations and storage. Forming (2), its normalization row and (3) and solving them takes \(O(n^3)\). No numerical test is used to establish these asymptotic statements.

Nonvacuity. For the general recovery budget and explicit Hermitian budget, (4) gives a family for every \(s=2^r\ge4\). At \(s=4\), symbolic substitution gives \(n=63,g=6,m=15,t=7,a=19\), message dimension ten and margin \(63-(19+15+7)=22\). These are arithmetic consequences of (4), not reported computer output. The message space includes one, and every error pattern of the stated size is covered. The genus and length grow unboundedly, and \(s^3-1>s^2+1\), so this is not the previous projective-line evaluation construction on the same alphabet. No claim of a new code or superior decoding radius is made.

Degenerate cases. At \(t=0\), the proof still works; the homogeneous variant has a nonzero solution and the marked variant has its normalized solution. The zero message is recovered uniquely because the product argument uses \(\lambda\ne0\), not \(f\ne0\). Empty coordinate sets cannot satisfy (1). Repeated points violate the distinct-point hypothesis and cannot be counted as extra zeros. A protected point in the error support is excluded; the algorithm does not promise normalization there. Divisors meeting evaluation or protected points are excluded because ordinary rational-function evaluation would not supply the stated coordinates. Genus zero specializes to the recorded polynomial locator mechanism rather than a new result. A nonzero locator may vanish on some correct coordinates; recovery still follows from the whole product identity, not coordinatewise division.

Source calibration and remaining scope

The author survey Høholdt–van Lint–Pellikaan, Algebraic geometry codes (1998), Theorem 2.55, Corollary 2.58, Example 2.74, Definitions 6.2–6.3, Lemma 6.4 and Proposition 6.5 with its proof, was read locally from the identified PDF. Its basic decoder already has the genus-loss phenomenon and cubic linear-algebra cost. The present proof uses evaluation codes directly and separately charges the prescribed-value condition. Pellikaan (1996), Definition 2.1, Remark 2.2 and Section 4, was also read: Proposition 4.2 gives stronger extension-field existence, while the paragraph after Remark 4.3 explicitly distinguishes existence from finding the pair efficiently. Neither it nor later decoding algorithms are claimed to be improved here. The section's extension-field bound is not used. The standard Riemann–Roch and smooth-plane genus theorems are imports; the repair and explicit-family deductions are proved above.

New mathematics. A full marked-value curve-recovery application of the recorded locator and bilinear tools, with explicit Hermitian input construction and all pole costs. The mathematical ingredients and basic decoder are established; this is a new notebook application and costed synthesis, with no historical novelty claim.

Selection gain. The previously supplied known-defect locator becomes an observable unknown-defect construction, and a marked point supplies an explicit protected value. The genus and zero bounds independently certify selection and recovery. No Pang contraction improvement or circuit-hardness conclusion is obtained.

Common marked locator for overlapping supports below the summed-rank budget

16 October 2026 · Working proof, elementary union-of-supports scope · Overlapping curve recovery and exact selection. Produced by claude-code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning medium).

Chosen. Continue the curve lead on effective-selection and joint-error-savings, following its latest development: “Develop the partially overlapping-support construction with distinct incomparable kernels”. The notebook's goal statement orders the four objectives, “Pursue them in that order”, and its Route decisions record that the user requires all four; joint-error-savings is the first open one. The missing implication is a strict joint-action saving that survives the full locator, observation and recovery budget; merely changing the values on one support would leave the same kernel.

Owed attempt. The preceding entry's reviewer suggestion, verified there by the author (Reviewer's suggestions of the marked-recovery entry), named this construction with simultaneous received-word systems and an explicit sum-of-ranks baseline. It is made below. This cycle also proves the selection-step consequence that the review of the first draft found to be a few lines, instead of deferring it.

General statement. thm:overlapping-curve-recovery gives the exact joint error-action rank on a common geometric domain, simultaneous marked recovery, and an unbounded Hermitian family where the summed-rank sufficient criterion fails at a working pole budget. lem:exact-common-kernel-selection replaces the summed rank condition of the selection step in thm:certified-repair-composition by an exact criterion on the stacked observation map. In the evaluation model the joint rank is the size of the union of supports by definition: this is the elementary common-locator mechanism of interleaved decoding, not a new decoding algorithm or a bound on the joint action of paid proof errors.

Exact common-kernel selection

Statement. Let \(S\) be a finite-dimensional space over a field, \(J\le S\) a subspace, \(\rho_a:S\to W_a\) finitely many linear maps, \(\rho=(\rho_a)_a\) the stacked map and \(q:S\to S/J\) the quotient. Then \(\bigcap_a\ker\rho_a\not\subseteq J\) if and only if

\[\operatorname{rank}(\rho,q)>\operatorname{rank}\rho.\tag{0}\]

In particular (0) holds whenever \(\operatorname{rank}\rho<\dim S/J\), and so whenever \(\sum_ar_a<\dim S/J\) for upper bounds \(r_a\ge\operatorname{rank}\rho_a\). A basis of \(\ker\rho\) and reduction modulo \(J\) find a vector of \(\ker\rho\setminus J\) when (0) holds.

Proof. \(\ker(\rho,q)=\ker\rho\cap J\). Rank–nullity on \(S\) for both maps gives \(\dim\ker\rho-\dim(\ker\rho\cap J)=\operatorname{rank}(\rho,q)-\operatorname{rank}\rho\). The left side is positive exactly when \(\ker\rho\not\subseteq J\). For the sufficient forms, \(\operatorname{rank}(\rho,q)\ge\operatorname{rank}q=\dim S/J\), and \(\operatorname{rank}\rho\le\sum_a\operatorname{rank}\rho_a\le\sum_ar_a\).

Use in the composition. In thm:certified-repair-composition take \(J=S\cap J_D\). Its proof uses the summed bound only to obtain some \(w\in\bigcap_a\ker\rho_a\setminus J_D\); the exact-repair construction, the finite-grid stage, the absorption of earlier errors and replay all start from that chosen \(w\), as its full proof states. So the composition holds verbatim with its hypothesis \(\sum_ar_a<H_0\) replaced by any certificate of (0). This changes the selection hypothesis, not its later costs. A certificate of (0) is itself a cost: an independent bound on each \(\rho_a\) does not bound the stacked rank better than the sum, and for Braun's and Pang's adapters no joint bound is recorded. The curve theorem below supplies one in its own model. It is a linear-algebra refinement; by its registered statement, read in this cycle, lem:weight-compatibility-factorization already uses the predicate “common kernel nonzero in the quotient by the forbidden space” for two families.

Overlapping curve recovery

Setting and statement. Use a smooth projective geometrically integral curve \(X/k\) of genus \(g\) over a finite field, distinct rational received points \(P_1,\ldots,P_n\), a further protected rational point \(R\), and divisors \(A,G\) of nonnegative degrees \(a,m\) supported away from all these points. There are \(L\ge1\) received words

\[y^{(j)}=\operatorname{ev}(f_j)+e^{(j)},\qquad f_j\in L(G).\]

Let \(E_j\) be the exact nonzero support of \(e^{(j)}\), \(E=\bigcup_jE_j\), \(u=|E|\), \(t=\max_j|E_j|\). Assume

\[a\ge u+2g,\qquad n>a+m+t.\tag{1}\]

All ranks below use the same domain \(U=L(A)\). Define \(T_j(\lambda)=(\lambda(P_i)e_i^{(j)})_i\), \(H=\operatorname{ev}L(A+G)\), and the quotient map \(Q:k^n\to k^n/H\). Then

\[\operatorname{rank}T_j=\operatorname{rank}(QT_j)=|E_j|,\qquad \operatorname{rank}(T_1,\ldots,T_L)=\operatorname{rank}(QT_1,\ldots,QT_L)=u.\tag{2}\]

The common kernel is \(L(A-\sum_{i\in E}P_i)\), and evaluation at \(R\) on it is surjective. Thus a common locator \(\lambda(R)=1\) exists. It is found without knowing the supports by solving

\[\lambda\in L(A),\quad N_j\in L(A+G),\quad N_j(P_i)=\lambda(P_i)y_i^{(j)}\quad\forall i,j,\qquad\lambda(R)=1.\tag{3}\]

Every solution satisfies \(N_j=\lambda f_j\) identically and kills every actual error. Separate linear systems with this same locator recover all \(f_j\) and their protected values \(f_j(R)=N_j(R)\). With bases and evaluations, including the \(R\)-evaluation row, supplied, the algorithm uses \(O(Ln^3)\) field operations and \(O(n^2+Ln)\) memory. It may use supplied upper bounds \(u_0\ge u,t_0\ge t\) in (1); the actual supports need not be supplied or guessed. Finiteness of \(k\) is inherited from the dependency's statement and is not used by the new steps; the exact ranks need only \(a\ge u+2g-1\), and a nonzero common locator only \(a\ge u+g\); the full (1) is used for normalization at \(R\).

Proof of the rank identities. Since \(a-u-1\ge2g-1\), Riemann–Roch and the negative-degree dual-section criterion give \(H^1(A-\sum_{i\in E}P_i-R)=0\). The evaluation exact sequence makes

\[L(A)\longrightarrow k^E\oplus k,\qquad \lambda\longmapsto((\lambda(P_i))_{i\in E},\lambda(R))\tag{4}\]

surjective. This is the point-evaluation argument in the full proof of cor:curve-locator-budget, now for the whole finite set. Multiplication by an error factors through evaluation on its support, followed by an injective diagonal map because all its values there are nonzero. This proves the individual ranks. The stacked map factors through evaluation on \(E\), followed by an injective map: each union coordinate has a nonzero error value in at least one component, so an element killed in every component has every coordinate zero. Its rank is therefore \(u\), and its kernel is the stated vanishing space. Surjectivity in (4) supplies the normalized locator. In the language of lem:exact-common-kernel-selection with \(S=U\), \(J=\{\lambda:\lambda(R)=0\}\) and \(\rho=(T_j)_j\), (4) says \(\operatorname{rank}(\rho,q)=u+1>u=\operatorname{rank}\rho\), although \(\dim S/J=1\le u\): the sufficient forms of that lemma fail here and only the exact criterion applies.

A nonzero element of \(H\) has at least \(n-a-m>t\) nonzero coordinates, by the divisor-zero proof in thm:marked-curve-recovery. Thus \(Q\) is injective on vectors supported on any \(E_j\); applying it separately to the components preserves each individual kernel and the common kernel. Rank–nullity proves both observable identities in (2). All comparisons use \(U\), not separate quotients chosen for separate errors.

Proof of recovery and cost. Eliminating \(N_j\), a \(\lambda\in L(A)\) extends to a solution of (3) exactly when \(Q\operatorname{diag}(y^{(j)})\operatorname{ev}_A\lambda=0\) for every \(j\). Since \(\operatorname{ev}(\lambda f_j)\in H\), \(Q\operatorname{diag}(\operatorname{ev}f_j)\operatorname{ev}_A=0\), hence \(Q\operatorname{diag}(y^{(j)})\operatorname{ev}_A=QT_j\): the observable map of row \(j\) is \(QT_j\). A locator from (4) vanishing on \(E\), together with \(N_j=\lambda f_j\), solves (3), so that system is consistent. For any solution, \(N_j-\lambda f_j\) vanishes at the at least \(n-|E_j|>a+m\) correct positions in row \(j\); the same zero bound makes it identically zero. Since \(\lambda(R)=1\), it is nonzero. Recover each \(f_j\) by the second system of thm:marked-curve-recovery; its uniqueness proof applies verbatim to each row.

For the runtime bound, do not eliminate a single dense matrix with \(L\) separate numerator blocks cubically in its full width. First form \(Q\) once by elimination on the supplied evaluation basis of \(H\). In row \(j\) the observable map \(Q\,\mathrm{diag}(y^{(j)})\operatorname{ev}_A\) has at most \(n\) rows and \(a+1=O(n)\) columns. Form it in \(O(n^3)\) field operations and insert its rows into a maintained echelon system for \(\lambda\), including \(\lambda(R)=1\). Across all rows this costs \(O(Ln^3)\). The consistent affine solution is a locator. For each word solve \(\lambda(P_i)h(P_i)=\lambda(P_i)y_i^{(j)}\) in \(L(G)\), another \(O(n^3)\) operations. Compute \(N_j=\lambda h\), or just its evaluations and protected value, after recovering \(h\). Streaming the constraint rows requires \(O(n^2)\) workspace; retaining the received words uses \(O(Ln)\). Product coordinates, if requested, use the supplied multiplication representation or an additional solve in the injective evaluation basis of \(H\), again cubic per word. No search through supports is used.

One protected quotient

Let \(\mathcal A=k^{\{P_1,\ldots,P_n,R\}}\), with coordinate multiplication, and let \(\operatorname{ev}_{D+R}\) be the evaluation map from functions regular at these points. Its kernel is one fixed ideal. Its restriction to \(U=L(A)\) is injective, because a nonzero element of \(L(A)\) has at most \(a<n\) zeros. This identifies \(U\) with its image in that same algebra for every action in (2). Extend each error by zero at \(R\), and let \(p_R\) be the coordinate idempotent there. The selected multiplier obeys \(\lambda e^{(j)}=0\) for all \(j\) and \(\lambda p_R=p_R\). The \(R\)-coordinate is not discarded in a quotient or changed between rows. The tester quotient \(k^n/H\) is only an observation space, not the protected algebra. This section records the bookkeeping; it adds no constraint beyond (1).

Strict savings at unbounded parameters

Family. On the same Hermitian curves, received points and protected point as cor:hermitian-repair-family, take every \(s=2^r\ge8\) and put

\[n=s^3-1,\quad g=s(s-1)/2,\quad m=s^3/4-1,\quad d=s^3/16-1,\quad a=3d+2g,\quad A=aP_\infty,\ G=mP_\infty.\tag{5}\]

Choose disjoint sets \(I,J,K\) of received points, each of size \(d\), and take two error vectors with nonzero values exactly on \(E_1=I\cup J\) and \(E_2=I\cup K\). Arbitrary nonzero values are permitted. The decoder uses only \(u_0=3d,t_0=2d\). There are enough points since \(3d<n\). The supports and action kernels are distinct and incomparable: surjectivity in (4) gives a multiplier nonzero at a chosen point of \(J\) and zero on \(I\cup K\), and conversely for \(K\). Thus neither individual kernel contains the other.

The ranks are \(2d,2d\) and \(3d\) jointly. This is genuine overlap between different constraints on one domain, not copies of one error map or an invertible change of its output coordinates. It is elementary support geometry: the structural hypothesis, a bound on the union of supports, is in this model the joint-rank bound itself, so the saving over the sum is inclusion–exclusion. It supplies no bound on arbitrary PC error images.

Why one multiplier. Each word alone has a normalized locator at pole order \(2d+2g<a\), cheaper than both budgets below. Separate locators give separate multipliers. The selection step of thm:certified-repair-composition needs one weight \(w\) killing every defect while preserving the protected class, so the comparison below is between certificates for one common multiplier. The curve instance is an instance of lem:exact-common-kernel-selection only and an analogue of that selection step: the composition is stated for polynomial spaces \(S\le k[x]_{\le b}\) and a cutoff space \(J_D\), and no translation carrying \(L(A)\), its forbidden hyperplane \(\lambda(R)=0\) and its observations into that setting is given here.

Budget comparison. Since \(a\ge2g\), Riemann–Roch gives \(\ell(A)=a+1-g=3d+g+1\). Moreover

\[d-g-1=s^3/16-s(s-1)/2-2 =s^2(s-8)/16+s/2-2>0\quad(s\ge8).\tag{6}\]

Thus \(3d<\ell(A)<4d\). For a pole order \(b\), the individual rank of the action of \(e^{(j)}\) on \(L(bP_\infty)\) is \(\ell(bP_\infty)-\ell(bP_\infty-E_j)\), so the summed-rank criterion for a nonzero common multiplier reads \(\ell(bP_\infty)<\ell(bP_\infty-E_1)+\ell(bP_\infty-E_2)\).

  • For \(b\ge2d+2g-1\) both sides are nonspecial: \(\ell(bP_\infty-E_j)=b-2d+1-g\), \(\ell(bP_\infty)=b+1-g\), and the criterion holds exactly when \(b\ge b_{\rm sum}=4d+g\).
  • For \(b<2d\) the right side is zero and the criterion fails.
  • For \(2d\le b\le4d-g-1\), the upper bound \(\ell(D)\le\deg D+1\) for \(\deg D\ge0\) (used in thm:marked-curve-recovery) gives a right side at most \(2b-4d+2\le b+1-g\le\ell(bP_\infty)\), and the criterion fails.

The last two ranges and the first cover every \(b\) when \(4d-g-1\ge2d+2g-2\), that is \(2d\ge3g-1\); here \(2d-3g+1=s^2(s-12)/8+3s/2-1>0\) for \(s\ge16\). So for every \(s\ge16\) and every choice of \(I,J,K\), the exact summed-rank criterion first holds at \(b_{\rm sum}=4d+g\). At \(s=8\) the orders \(96\le b\le116\) are not covered by this argument; there the comparison is with the support-based rank bounds \(2d,2d\), which are exact at every \(b\ge2d+2g-1\) and valid upper bounds below, and the same threshold \(4d+g\) is that criterion's first order.

Two comparisons follow, with the same curve, point at infinity, supports and message divisor.

  • Nonzero against nonzero. The actual common kernel \(L(bP_\infty-E)\) is nonzero once \(b\ge3d+g\), by Riemann–Roch, against \(b_{\rm sum}=4d+g\): a saving of \(d\). At \(s=8\) this is established against the support-based rank bounds only, because of the uncovered orders above.
  • Protected against the summed baseline. The composition needs \(w\) outside the forbidden space, here \(\lambda(R)\ne0\). The summed and joint rank conditions of the composition both ask for a rank below \(\dim S/J=1\) and never hold. The exact criterion (0) holds at \(a=3d+2g\) by (4). Against the summed nonzero threshold, which gives no normalization at all, the saving is \(b_{\rm sum}-a=d-g>0\).

Neither \(3d+g\) nor \(a\) is asserted optimal, and other decoders are not compared. The saving survives detection and message recovery, because

\[n-(a+m+2d)=7s^3/16-s^2+s+5>0.\tag{7}\]

The baseline's larger pole budget also fits the observation window: \(n-(b_{\rm sum}+m+2d)=3s^3/8-s^2/2+s/2+6>0\). Thus the comparison is between two feasible pole windows, not with an impossible baseline. The weighted-message budget drops by the same \(d-g\), from \(b_{\rm sum}+m\) to \(a+m\). Points, bases and evaluation matrices use the explicit construction already proved in cor:hermitian-repair-family, at the new \(O(n)\) pole orders. For two words the complete cost remains \(O(n^3)\) field operations and \(O(n^2)\) memory.

Nonvacuity. The same-quotient protected ledger and full saving and recovery ledger are both realized by (5) for every \(s=2^r\ge8\). At \(s=8\), direct symbolic substitution gives \(n=511,g=28,m=127,d=31,a=149\), ranks \(62,62,93\), domain dimension \(122\), summed-rank threshold \(b_{\rm sum}=152\), and recovery margin \(511-(149+127+62)=173\). At \(s=16\): \(n=4095,g=120,d=255,a=1005,b_{\rm sum}=1140\). One may take both messages equal to one and each error equal to one on its prescribed support; the supports remain distinct. These are exact substitutions, not a numerical run. For lem:exact-common-kernel-selection, the same instance gives \(S=L(A)\) of dimension \(122\), \(\operatorname{rank}\rho=93\), \(\operatorname{rank}(\rho,q)=94\).

Degenerate cases. If every error is zero, the joint rank is zero and a normalized locator exists under (1). With one word there is no rank-sum gain. Disjoint supports give equality between union and summed ranks. Identical supports may have different nonzero values but the same kernel; they are not the claimed nonduplicate family. A zero error value removes that coordinate from its exact support, so it is not counted twice by definition. Repeated evaluation points are excluded. A protected point in the defect support is excluded. In lem:exact-common-kernel-selection, \(J=S\) makes (0) impossible, \(J=0\) reduces it to \(\ker\rho\ne0\), and no maps at all give \(\rho=0\) and the condition \(S\ne J\).

New mathematics. A complete simultaneous marked-recovery consequence with exact joint-action ranks; a pole-budget gain of \(d\) (nonzero) and \(d-g\) (protected) over the summed-rank certificate on distinct incomparable kernels, exact for every support choice when \(s\ge16\); and an exact selection criterion for the composition theorem. The curve instance satisfies that criterion, an instance of the abstract lemma where its summed form cannot certify a protected value; it is not itself an instance of the composition theorem. The support factorization and the decoder are established elementary tools; no historical novelty or improved decoding radius is claimed. Simultaneous decoding of several received words with one error locator for the union of their supports is the setting of interleaved codes; no source on interleaved decoding was read in this cycle, and nothing here relies on one.

Selection gain. lem:exact-common-kernel-selection widens the selection hypothesis of the composition from a summed rank bound to an exact rank criterion, with unchanged later costs. In the explicit family a protected common multiplier is certified at pole budget \(3d+2g\), where the summed criterion first gives even a nonzero multiplier at \(4d+g\). This changes the sufficient common-multiplier cost, not only its representation. Separate per-word locators are cheaper still; the gain concerns one common multiplier.

Goal progress. Unchanged. In the evaluation model, thm:overlapping-curve-recovery gives same-quotient distinct kernels and an end-to-end pole saving, but the saving is union-of-supports counting: there the joint rank is the union size by definition. The necessary implication of joint-error-savings, a structural bound on the joint action of actual errors from independent hypotheses, is not touched; lem:exact-common-kernel-selection widens a hypothesis of the composition, a stronger convenience rather than such a bound. Bearing. The protected quotient retains the same coordinate throughout, and the saving ledger pays observation and both recovery steps at unbounded parameters. Not covered. Arbitrary PC errors or Frege proofs, Braun's and Pang's adapters, optimal decoding algorithms, effective homology/localization bounds or circuit hardness.

Record search. Searches covered simultaneous curve recovery and common locators with union supports, interleaved joint error locators with overlapping supports and incomparable kernels, and exact common-kernel selection by a stacked-observation rank criterion. They returned cor:curve-locator-budget, cor:hermitian-repair-family, prop:multiplier-transfer-limits, lem:weight-compatibility-factorization and unrelated feature-transfer results, but no matched curve-budget or exact-selection statement. The resemblance check on the claim spec returned thm:marked-curve-recovery (0.29), cor:hermitian-repair-family (0.28) and cor:curve-locator-budget (0.17), all related in the spec, and the queued sub-idea that this entry develops. The full proof of thm:certified-repair-composition was reread for the selection step.

Affine exclusion counted by shared input subspaces

16 October 2026 · Working proof, elementary; extends Braun Section 5 · Shared-span affine exclusion. Produced by claude-code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning medium).

Chosen. This entry takes the queued Mayer–Vietoris lead rather than the head, entry-2026-10-15-multiplier-separation:s1. Both bear on joint-error-savings, whose necessary implication is a structural bound on the joint action of actual errors that beats the sum of their ranks in one quotient. The head's two developments, two-sided Frobenius and product-code comparison, found no product-code estimate of the actual error action, and the latter moved to a different mechanism; the head's current spell has no entry yet, so it is not overtaken by a spell rule, only by this choice. The Mayer–Vietoris lead comes first because it concerns ordinary restrictions, which are Braun's actual observations, so a bound there is a bound on actual errors with a published ledger to test against; the head's latest development tested product-code dimension theorems, which bound product spaces from below, not an actual error family. The preceding entry found that support counting in an evaluation model does not supply such a bound, so this cycle tests the overlap mechanism on Braun's published restriction count.

Owed attempt. This lead has no earlier development. Its review named the break point: “Exactness of the unfiltered scheme sequence does not imply exactness for an arbitrary selected low-degree domain”. It asked for the degreewise ideal-sum condition, “not naive inclusion-exclusion on arbitrary overlaps”. That break point concerns exactness. The direction selection needs is an upper bound on the joint rank, which needs no exactness; the lemma below records it, with an increment form (4) that bounds increments between blocks with no common containing subspace; its saving is untested (Follow-up). The theorem uses only the containment form (3).

General statement. lem:restriction-rank-overlap bounds the joint rank of restriction maps on an arbitrary finite-dimensional space of polynomials through overlaps and containing ideals, with no exactness hypothesis. thm:shared-span-affine-exclusion applies the containment half to Braun's affine exclusion. Its inventory hypothesis, at most \(M\) proper high-rank blocks, becomes a hypothesis on the number \(C\) of shared subspaces covering their input spans. Every other part of the published ledger is unchanged.

Restriction rank overlap

Statement. Let \(R\) be a commutative ring containing a field \(\mathbb K\), \(U\le R\) a finite-dimensional \(\mathbb K\)-subspace, and for an ideal \(I\) put \(r(I)=\dim U-\dim(U\cap I)\), the rank of \(U\to R/I\). Then for ideals \(I\subseteq I'\) and \(I,J\):

\[r(I')\le r(I),\qquad r(I\cap J)\le r(I)+r(J)-r(I+J).\tag{1}\]

For ideals \(I_1,\ldots,I_M\), the stacked map \(U\to\bigoplus_aR/I_a\) has rank \(r(\bigcap_aI_a)\), and

\[r\Bigl(\bigcap_aI_a\Bigr)\le\sum_{a=1}^Mr(I_a)-\sum_{a=2}^Mr(I_a+I_{b(a)})\tag{2}\]

for every choice of indices \(b(a)<a\). If ideals \(K_1,\ldots,K_C\) satisfy: every \(I_a\) contains some \(K_c\), then

\[r\Bigl(\bigcap_aI_a\Bigr)\le r\Bigl(\bigcap_cK_c\Bigr)\le\sum_{c=1}^Cr(K_c).\tag{3}\]

Increments are monotone in the space: for \(U\le P\) finite-dimensional, \(r_U(I)-r_U(I+J)\le r_P(I)-r_P(I+J)\). For affine flats, let \(R=\mathbb K[x_1,\ldots,x_v]\), \(P=R_{\le k}\), and for an affine span \(S\) let \((S)\) be the ideal it generates in \(R\), not the ideal of polynomials vanishing on the flat's points. If \(1\notin S_a+S_b\), \(\dim S_a=r_a\), \(d_a=v-r_a\) and \(c=\dim(S_a+S_b)-r_a\), then

\[r_U((S_a))-r_U((S_a+S_b))\le\binom{d_a+k}{k}-\binom{d_a-c+k}{k}\le c\binom{d_a+k-1}{k-1}=\frac{ck}{d_a+k}\binom{d_a+k}{k}.\tag{4}\]

So with (2), \(r_U(\bigcap_a(S_a))\le r_U((S_1))+\sum_{a\ge2}\min\{r_U((S_a)),\,c_a\binom{d_a+k-1}{k-1}\}\), where \(c_a\) is the increment of \(S_{b(a)}\) over \(S_a\), and the minimum is \(r_U((S_a))\) when \(1\in S_a+S_{b(a)}\).

Proof. Monotonicity holds because \(U\cap I\subseteq U\cap I'\). The stacked kernel is \(U\cap\bigcap_aI_a\). For (1), \((U\cap I)+(U\cap J)\subseteq U\cap(I+J)\) and \((U\cap I)\cap(U\cap J)=U\cap(I\cap J)\), so

\[\dim U\cap(I\cap J)=\dim U\cap I+\dim U\cap J-\dim\bigl((U\cap I)+(U\cap J)\bigr)\ge\dim U\cap I+\dim U\cap J-\dim U\cap(I+J).\]

Subtracting from \(\dim U\) gives (1). For (2), induct on \(M\) with \(I=I_1\cap\cdots\cap I_{a-1}\), \(J=I_a\): (1) gives \(r(I\cap I_a)\le r(I)+r(I_a)-r(I+I_a)\), and \(I+I_a\subseteq I_{b(a)}+I_a\), so monotonicity gives \(r(I+I_a)\ge r(I_{b(a)}+I_a)\). Any \(b(a)<a\) works because \(I_1\cap\cdots\cap I_{a-1}\subseteq I_{b(a)}\). For (3), \(\bigcap_cK_c\subseteq\bigcap_aI_a\) gives the first inequality by monotonicity, and (2) with \(b\) arbitrary, dropping the nonnegative subtracted terms, gives the second. For the increment, \(r_U(I)-r_U(I+J)=\dim\,(U\cap(I+J))/(U\cap I)\), and this quotient maps injectively to \((P\cap(I+J))/(P\cap I)\), because \(U\cap(P\cap I)=U\cap I\). For (4), in affine coordinates adapted to \(S_a\subseteq S_a+S_b\) (the published affine-coordinates lemma, using \(1\notin S_a+S_b\)), \(P\cap(S)\) is spanned by the monomials of degree at most \(k\) divisible by one of the first \(\dim S\) coordinates, so \(r_P((S_a))=\binom{d_a+k}{k}\) and \(r_P((S_a+S_b))=\binom{d_a-c+k}{k}\). Pascal's rule writes the difference as \(\sum_{i=1}^{c}\binom{d_a+k-i}{k-1}\le c\binom{d_a+k-1}{k-1}\).

Scope. (1) is the standard submodularity of \(I\mapsto\dim(U\cap I)\), and the lemma is elementary. For subsets of the Boolean cube and \(U\) the space of all degree-\(d\) functions, (1) together with \(I_U+I_W\subseteq I_{U\cap W}\) and monotonicity implies the “always \(\le0\)” clause of the registered lem:degreewise-gluing; equality there is its gluing condition. Here \(U\) is arbitrary and restrictions are ordinary, through ideals, so the lemma also covers Braun's row-linear space \(\mathcal L_k\) and literal affine restrictions. An upper bound on the joint rank is what lem:exact-common-kernel-selection and Braun's dimension count need; no exactness is used. Form (4) avoids lower bounds on intersection ranks by comparing increments with the full degree-\(k\) space; the theorem below uses only (3).

Shared-span affine exclusion

Setting. Use the publication's Section 5 exactly. Over \(\mathbb F_2\), take \(m>0\), \(\ell\ge2\), \(n=2^\ell\), \(h=3\ell\), \(v=m\ell\) old bits, the old system \(\mathcal Q_{m,\ell}\), and a finite complete old-affine family of accuracy-\(h\) blocks with disjoint fresh coefficients. The input span \(S_a\) of a block is the span of its affine inputs; rank is \(\dim S_a\); the block is proper if \(S_a\) is nonzero and \(1\notin S_a\). \(\mathcal L_k\) is the row-linear space of the publication's Section 4: the span of the monomials of degree at most \(k\) containing at most one bit from each row, of dimension \(\sum_{j\le k}\binom mj\ell^j\ge\binom mk\ell^k\). For \(k\ge1\) it contains the constants, every bit and so every linear form. \(B=k(D+1)\). The ideal of a flat below is the ideal generated in \(\mathbb F_2[x]\) by its affine span, so restriction is ordinary substitution, not evaluation at Boolean points.

Statement. Let \(k\ge1\) and \(C\ge1\). Suppose there are subspaces \(T_1,\ldots,T_C\) of old affine polynomials, each of dimension greater than \(h(k+1)\), such that the input span of every proper block of rank greater than \(h(k+1)\) contains some \(T_c\). If

\[m\ln(4C)\le k^2,\quad k\le m,\quad D\ge2h+1,\quad2B-1\le n,\quad4(k-1)<n,\tag{6}\]

the augmented system has no ordinary-PC refutation through degree \(D\). The number of blocks is not bounded. Taking \(T\) to be the spans of the proper high-rank blocks themselves gives \(C\le M\), so this contains the published finite-parameter affine exclusion theorem. For fixed natural \(a,A,d\) and every sufficiently large \(\ell\), with \(m=n+1\) and \(k=\lceil(\ell+1)\sqrt2^{\,\ell}\rceil\), the \(k\) of the uniform eventual parameter lemma at constants \(a,A+7,d+1\): if every proper block of rank greater than \(3\ell(k+1)\) contains one of at most \(A2^{a\ell}+1\) subspaces of dimension greater than \(3\ell(k+1)\), the family has no PC refutation of degree \(D\le A(\ell+1)^d\).

Proof of the common kernel. If no proper block has rank greater than \(h(k+1)\), take \(f=1\), as in the published kernel lemma. Otherwise discard every \(T_c\) contained in no proper high-rank span; such a \(T_c\) may contain one, and discarding only lowers \(C\). Each remaining \(T_c\) lies inside a proper span, so \(1\notin T_c\), and by the published affine-coordinates lemma it has a nonempty zero flat \(W_c\) of codimension \(\dim T_c\ge r_*=3\ell(k+1)+1\). If \(r_*>v\) no remaining subspace exists, and we are in the first case. Ordinary restriction to \(W_c\) maps \(\mathcal L_k\) into polynomials of degree at most \(k\) in \(v-\dim T_c\le d=v-r_*\) free coordinates, so its rank is at most \(\binom{d+k}{k}\). The published comparison gives \(\binom{d+k}{k}\le e^{-k^2/m}\binom mk\ell^k\). By (3) with \(K_c=(T_c)\), the ideal generated by \(T_c\), or directly, the joint restriction map to the \(W_c\) has rank at most

\[Ce^{-k^2/m}\binom mk\ell^k\le\tfrac14\binom mk\ell^k<\dim\mathcal L_k,\tag{5}\]

using \(e^{-k^2/m}\le1/(4C)\) from (6). So there is a nonzero \(f\in\mathcal L_k\) whose ordinary restriction to every \(W_c\) is the zero polynomial.

Proof that every block is covered. Let a proper block of rank \(r_a>h(k+1)\) have \(T_c\subseteq S_a\). Choose a basis \(F_1,\ldots,F_t\) of \(T_c\) and extend it to a basis \(F_1,\ldots,F_{r_a}\) of \(S_a\). By the affine-coordinates lemma, applied to \(S_a\) with \(1\notin S_a\), these extend to invertible affine coordinates of degree one. In them, restriction to \(W_c\) sets \(F_1,\ldots,F_t\) to zero and restriction to the zero flat \(Z_a\) of \(S_a\) sets \(F_1,\ldots,F_{r_a}\) to zero. A polynomial whose restriction to \(W_c\) is zero has every monomial divisible by some \(F_j\) with \(j\le t\), so its restriction to \(Z_a\) is zero too. Zero restriction does not depend on the chosen coordinates, because two affine parametrizations of one flat differ by an invertible affine change of its free coordinates. The published restriction-ideal lemma then gives the literal cofactors \(f=\sum_ia_{a,i}g_{a,i}\), \(\deg a_{a,i}\le k-1\), for that block.

Proof of exclusion. The published weighted-removal lemma needs only an ordinary \(f\) of degree \(s\le k\) and, for every block of rank greater than \(h(k+1)\), either one in its span or the literal cofactors. Both are now supplied. It gives \(f\in\mathrm{Cons}_{k(D+1)}(\mathcal Q_{m,\ell})\), contrary to the published row-separation theorem under \(2B-1\le n\) and \(4(k-1)<n\). In the published proofs the inventory \(M\) enters only the kernel count (5); the removal, packing, separation and replay steps are read from the publication's Section 5 and do not use it. For the eventual regime, the uniform eventual parameter lemma is a statement about the numbers \(n,k\) and \(A2^{a\ell}+1\) only; the polynomial-inventory corollary's degree raise to \(\max(D,6\ell+1)\) applies verbatim, with the shared-subspace count in place of the block count.

Strict inventory gain at unbounded parameters

Family. Fix \(\ell\ge16\), \(m=n+1\), \(k=\lceil\sqrt{m\ln4}\rceil\), and \(C=1\). Let \(T_1\) be the span of \(r_*=3\ell(k+1)+1\) old bits \(x_1,\ldots,x_{r_*}\). Put \(G=3\ell(n/4+2)+1\) and \(\rho=G-r_*=3\ell(n/4+1-k)\); here \(\rho\ge3\ell\), because \(4(k-1)<n\) and \(4\mid n\) give \(k\le n/4\), and \(v-G=\ell(n/4-5)-1\ge1\) for \(n\ge32\). For each \(\rho\)-dimensional subspace \(\Lambda\) of linear forms in the remaining \(v-r_*\) bits take a block whose inputs are \(x_1,\ldots,x_{r_*}\) and a basis of \(\Lambda\). Its span is \(T_1\oplus\Lambda\), of rank \(G\). The blocks are proper, because their inputs are linear forms, so their spans do not contain one. They are pairwise distinct, because projection to the remaining bits recovers \(\Lambda\). Their number is \(M\ge2^{\rho(v-G)}\), the standard lower bound for the number of \(\rho\)-dimensional subspaces of \(\mathbb F_2^{v-r_*}\). Every block contains \(T_1\).

Joint rank against the sum. In \(\mathcal L_k\), the restriction to each block's flat \(Z_\Lambda\), of dimension \(v-G\ge1\), has rank at least \(v-G+1\), since the restrictions of the constants and of the old bits span all affine functions on \(Z_\Lambda\). So the sum of the individual ranks is at least \(M\ge2^{\rho(v-G)}\). By (3) the joint rank is at most the rank of the restriction to \(W_1\), at most \(\tfrac14\binom mk\ell^k\le(m\ell)^k\) by (5). At \(\ell=16\), \(\log_2\) of the sum is at least \(\rho(v-G)=771984\cdot262063\) and \(\log_2\) of the joint bound at most \(302\cdot\log_2 1048592<302\cdot21=6342\). The kernels are distinct and incomparable. By the affine-coordinates lemma, a linear form has zero restriction to \(Z_\Lambda\) exactly when it lies in \(T_1\oplus\Lambda\). For \(\Lambda\ne\Lambda'\) of equal dimension, a form in \(\Lambda\setminus\Lambda'\) lies in \(\mathcal L_k\), vanishes on \(Z_\Lambda\) and not on \(Z_{\Lambda'}\).

Comparison with the published hypothesis. The published finite-parameter theorem may be applied with any \(k'\) satisfying its conditions. They include \(4(k'-1)<n\), so \(k'\le n/4\), and then \(h(k'+1)\le3\ell(n/4+1)<G\). So at every admissible \(k'\) all \(M\) blocks are proper and of rank above the threshold, and the theorem needs \(m\ln(4M)\le k'^2\le n^2/16\). But \(\ln M\ge\rho(v-G)\ln2\ge3\ell\,(\ell(n/4-5)-1)\ln2\), using \(v-G=\ell(n/4-5)-1\). For \(\ell\ge16\) this exceeds \(n/16\ge n^2/(16m)\). The published inventory condition therefore fails at every admissible \(k'\). The shared-span condition with \(C=1\) needs only \(m\ln4\le k^2\), which holds by the choice of \(k\). This compares stated hypotheses only; it does not show that no other argument excludes this family.

Degree. At \(\ell=16\): \(n=65536\), \(m=65537\), \(m\ln4\approx90853.58\), \(k=302\) with \(k^2=91204\), \(4(k-1)=1204<n\), \(h=48\), \(r_*=48\cdot303+1=14545\), \(v=1048592\), \(G=48\cdot16386+1=786529\), \(\rho=771984=48\cdot16083\), \(v-G=262063\). Condition \(2k(D+1)-1\le n\) holds for \(D\le107\), since \(604\cdot108-1=65231\le n\), and \(D=107\ge2h+1=97\). So this family has no ordinary-PC refutation through degree \(107\). As \(\ell\) grows, \(k\) is of order \(\sqrt n\) and the admissible degree \(D+1\le(n+1)/(2k)\) is of order \(\sqrt n\) too; checking \(D\ge6\ell+1\) eventually is the same elementary comparison as in the published uniform parameter lemma. For comparison, the published polynomial-inventory corollary reaches polylogarithmic degree for polynomial block inventory.

Nonvacuity. The inventory ledger is realized by the family above at \(\ell=16\), with the parameters listed, and by the same construction at every sufficiently large \(\ell\). Condition (6) at \(\ell=16\) was checked term by term in the Degree paragraph. For lem:restriction-rank-overlap, \(U=\mathcal L_k\) with the generated block ideals \((T_1\oplus\Lambda)\) of this family and \(K_1=(T_1)\) satisfies (3) with joint rank at most \(\tfrac14\binom mk\ell^k\). The numbers are exact substitutions, except \(m\ln4\), which is rounded only for display; no numerical run was made.

Degenerate cases. With no proper high-rank block, the statement is the published case \(f=1\). With \(C=M\) and \(T_a=S_a\) it is the published finite-parameter theorem. A subspace \(T_c\) contained in no block's span is harmless: it only enlarges the count. Unit-span blocks are not covered by any \(T_c\) and are removed by constant substitution as in the publication. In the lemma, \(I=J\) gives equality in (1); \(I+J=R\) gives \(r(I+J)=0\) and the plain sum; \(M=1\) gives (2) as an identity. In (4), \(c=0\) means \(S_b\subseteq S_a\) and gives a zero increment, the containment case.

New mathematics. An elementary bound for joint restriction rank on an arbitrary polynomial space, with no exactness hypothesis: submodularity, its containment form, and an increment form (4) that compares overlaps with the full degree-\(k\) space. Braun's affine exclusion with its inventory of proper high-rank blocks replaced by the number of shared subspaces covering their spans. An explicit family at \(\ell=16\), and at all large \(\ell\), with \(C=1\) and more than \(2^{\rho(v-G)}\) blocks, outside the published theorem's stated inventory condition at every admissible \(k'\). The proofs are monotonicity and submodularity of a dimension and composition of affine restrictions; (1) is standard.

Selection gain. Under the shared-subspace hypothesis, the common-kernel selection of Braun's ledger is paid per shared subspace rather than per block: the joint restriction rank is bounded by \(C\) restriction ranks of the shared flats instead of \(M\) block ranks. On the family above this replaces a summed bound of at least \(2^{\rho(v-G)}\) by one below \((m\ell)^k\). The admissible inventory is unbounded only for families clustered in this way; for a generic family \(C\) is close to \(M\) and nothing changes.

Goal progress. Unchanged. thm:shared-span-affine-exclusion bounds the joint action of Braun's actual restriction observations by a checkable structural hypothesis, but the gain is domination: every covered block's restriction factors through the restriction to one shared flat. The exclusion must hold for adversarially chosen families, generic families have \(C\) close to \(M\), and nothing here shows that the errors met on the goal's route have shared spans. So the necessary implication of joint-error-savings, a joint bound beating the sum for the errors that actually occur, is not established. Bearing. The inventory ledger gives the full exclusion at \(\ell=16\), degree \(107\), for a family of more than \(2^{\rho(v-G)}\) blocks with \(C=1\). Not covered. The worst case: families whose high-rank spans share no large common subspaces, where \(C\) is close to \(M\). Also Pang's approximation errors, and the all-prime and accuracy-dependent newer versions, which carry their own kernel counts and were read by statement only.

Record search. Searches for common-factor joint rank bounds, Castelnuovo–Mumford regularity, submodular restriction rank with union, intersection and Shearer, degreewise gluing and affine restriction unions, shared input spans with high-rank block clusters and affine inventory, and Braun's finite-parameter exclusion returned cor:feature-row-shortening (identical kernels for a common factor times features), lem:degreewise-gluing, the historical common-feature conditioning, and the registered Braun records lem:common-affine-restriction-kernel and audit:ordinary-restriction-affine-family. Their newer versions thm:all-prime-affine-family-exclusion and lem:accuracy-dependent-affine-exclusion were read by statement. Those searches returned no shared-span clustering of the inventory. The publication's Section 5 was read in full, and the definition and dimension lemma of \(\mathcal L_k\) in its Section 4. The resemblance check on the spec returned nothing above 0.25.

Verification gate. Dependencies read: the publication's Section 5 in full, the definition and dimension lemma of \(\mathcal L_k\) in its Section 4, and the registered statements of lem:degreewise-gluing, lem:exact-common-kernel-selection, lem:common-affine-restriction-kernel and cor:feature-row-shortening. The row-separation theorem of Section 4 is used only as the published exclusion proof uses it; its proof was not reread, and audit:ordinary-restriction-affine-family records it as Lean-verified with the kernel and removal steps. The sole fresh-context medium reviewer passed (1)–(3), the containment step, the fact that only the kernel count uses the inventory, all family and \(\ell=16\) numbers, and the comparison at every admissible \(k'\). It required generated ideals to be stated, the eventual clause to name its \(k\), unused \(T_c\) to be discarded, the gluing relation to be stated as an implication, and \(\mathcal L_k\) to be cited. It judged “advanced” overstated, since the gain is domination and adversarial families have \(C\) close to \(M\), and it suggested the increment form. In a follow-up check it passed (4) and its proof and required a renumbering, the case \(c=0\), and softer wording in Chosen and Owed attempt. All changes were made. No numerical run; no Lean verification. Review record. Process audit. The reviewer found the Mayer–Vietoris framing of the first draft overstated, because the theorem uses only containment, and the goal-progress flag stronger than the result; both were corrected. It found the final title “Affine exclusion counted by shared input subspaces”, which it proposed, accurate against the body.

A first witness used blocks of rank just above the threshold, which the published theorem would still exclude at \(k'=k+1\) by packing them as low-rank; this was found before the review and the witness rebuilt with rank above every admissible threshold. Theorem numbers for the publication were first written from memory and replaced by its theorem names before the review. For the second entry in a row the reviewer found goal progress overstated. Prevention. The reviewer's challenge of the goal-progress flag, which every brief requests, caught both overstatements, and whether a gain meets an open statement is a judgement no committed check can make; the repetition is carried to the scheduled review. Publication theorem numbers are now taken only from the source text, by name.

Next attempt: put (4) into Braun's kernel count along a spanning tree of the high-rank spans, giving the count \(1+\sum_{a\ge2}\min\{1,c_ak/(d+k)\}\) in place of \(M\), and test it on a drifting chain of spans with no large common subspace.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Replace the subtracted terms of (2) by increments bounded through the full degree-\(k\) space, along a spanning tree in span distance. This was verified by the author and adopted as (4) above, with the injection of quotients and Pascal's rule written out; its use in an exclusion is the next attempt. Record. A search made after the suggestion, for restriction rank increment, quotient injection, spanning tree, flats and binomial, returned only unrelated majority- and feature-transfer theorems; the earlier submodularity searches returned lem:degreewise-gluing. No increment bound of this form was returned.

Increment-count exclusion and a ceiling on the common-kernel step

16 October 2026 · Working proof, elementary; extends Braun Section 5 · Increment count and kernel ceiling. Produced by claude-code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning medium).

Chosen. This entry continues the Mayer–Vietoris lead, in its second entry of a four-entry spell, rather than the head entry-2026-10-15-multiplier-separation:s1. It bears on joint-error-savings: the preceding entry left a named attempt on Braun's actual restriction observations, and settling how far joint-rank savings can reach in that step decides whether the head's product-code question, which has no recorded error family, is worth taking next.

Previous development. The preceding entry named the next attempt: “put (4) into Braun's kernel count along a spanning tree of the high-rank spans”, test it on a drifting chain, and record the limit if the count cannot beat the inventory by more than a factor \((d+k)/k\). All three are below: the count is the theorem; its \((d+k)/k\) limit is proved for every cover; and the drifting chain tested, of rank \(r_*\), shows no gain, because it is low-rank at \(k+1\). The cycle adds a first-moment family that defeats every certificate of the common-kernel step at a fixed degree, for every degree parameter that degree allows.

General statement. thm:increment-count-exclusion is Braun's affine exclusion with the common-kernel count replaced by the increment bound of lem:restriction-rank-overlap along any ordering of covering subspaces; it contains the published theorem and thm:shared-span-affine-exclusion, and it improves the rank-aware sum of some cover by at most a factor \(\max_c(d_c+k)/k\). prop:common-kernel-ceiling gives, at each degree \(D\), coordinate blocks whose flats have no common nonzero kernel in \(\mathcal L_{k'}\) for every \(k'\) that \(D\) allows, with \(\ln M\) about \(3K_D^2/m+\ln(K_D\ln v)\), against the published \(\ln(4M)\le K_D^2/m\).

Increment count exclusion

Setting. As in shared-span affine exclusion: Braun's \(\mathbb F_2\) one-level complete accuracy-\(h\) old-affine family over \(\mathcal Q_{m,\ell}\), \(n=2^\ell\), \(h=3\ell\), \(v=m\ell\), the row-linear space \(\mathcal L_k\), \(B=k(D+1)\), and ideals generated by affine spans in \(\mathbb F_2[x]\).

Statement. Let \(k\ge1\). Let \(T_1,\ldots,T_C\) be subspaces of old affine polynomials with \(1\notin T_c\), of dimensions \(t_c\), such that the span of every proper block of rank greater than \(h(k+1)\) contains some \(T_c\). Order them and choose parents \(b(c)<c\). Put \(d_c=v-t_c\), and \(e_c=\dim(T_c+T_{b(c)})-t_c\) when \(1\notin T_c+T_{b(c)}\). Define

\[N=\binom{d_1+k}{k}+\sum_{c\ge2}\min\Bigl\{\binom{d_c+k}{k},\;e_c\binom{d_c+k-1}{k-1}\Bigr\},\tag{1}\]

where the term is \(\binom{d_c+k}{k}\) when \(1\in T_c+T_{b(c)}\). If \(N<\dim\mathcal L_k\), \(D\ge2h+1\), \(2B-1\le n\) and \(4(k-1)<n\), the augmented system has no ordinary-PC refutation through degree \(D\). If moreover every \(t_c>h(k+1)\) and \(k\le m\), then with \(d=v-r_*\), \(r_*=3\ell(k+1)+1\), it suffices that

\[m\ln(4C_{\rm inc})\le k^2,\qquad C_{\rm inc}=1+\sum_{c\ge2}\min\Bigl\{1,\frac{e_ck}{d+k}\Bigr\},\tag{2}\]

with the term \(1\) when \(1\in T_c+T_{b(c)}\).

Proof. With no proper high-rank block take \(f=1\). Otherwise discard every \(T_c\) contained in no proper high-rank span; this only removes terms of (1). Since \(1\notin T_c\), each flat \(W_c\) is nonempty of dimension \(d_c\). By (2) and (4) of lem:restriction-rank-overlap with \(U=\mathcal L_k\) and the ideals \((T_c)\), the joint restriction rank to the \(W_c\) is at most \(N\): the first ideal costs \(r_{\mathcal L_k}((T_1))\le\binom{d_1+k}{k}\), and each later one adds at most its increment over its parent, bounded by \(e_c\binom{d_c+k-1}{k-1}\) through the full degree-\(k\) space, or by its own rank \(\binom{d_c+k}{k}\). If \(N<\dim\mathcal L_k\) a nonzero \(f\in\mathcal L_k\) has zero restriction to every \(W_c\). The coverage, cofactor, weighted-removal and separation steps are those of the shared-span proof verbatim. For (2), \(t_c>h(k+1)\) gives \(d_c\le d\), and both \(\binom{d_c+k}{k}\) and \(\binom{d_c+k-1}{k-1}\) increase with \(d_c\), so \(N\le C_{\rm inc}\binom{d+k}{k}\) using \(\binom{d+k-1}{k-1}=\frac{k}{d+k}\binom{d+k}{k}\). The published comparison, which uses \(k\le m\), gives \(\binom{d+k}{k}\le e^{-k^2/m}\binom mk\ell^k\), and \(e^{-k^2/m}\le1/(4C_{\rm inc})\) gives \(N\le\frac14\binom mk\ell^k<\dim\mathcal L_k\).

Containment. Every term of \(C_{\rm inc}\) is at most \(1\), so \(C_{\rm inc}\le C\) and (2) is implied by the condition \(m\ln(4C)\le k^2\) of thm:shared-span-affine-exclusion, after the same discarding of unused subspaces; and (1) is at most the shared-span sum \(\sum_c\binom{d_c+k}{k}\). Taking each \(T_c\) to be a block span gives the published theorem.

Limit of the increment count. For any cover and any choice of parents, let \(S\) consist of index \(1\) and every \(c\) whose parent is not contained in \(T_c\). Every \(c\notin S\) contains its parent, so following parents it contains some member of \(S\); hence \(S\) is again a cover. For \(c\in S\), \(c\ge2\), either \(e_c\ge1\) or the term is \(\binom{d_c+k}{k}\), so each such term of (1) is at least \(\frac{k}{d_c+k}\binom{d_c+k}{k}\), and so is the first. So \(N\ge\min_c\frac{k}{d_c+k}\sum_{c\in S}\binom{d_c+k}{k}\): the increment count is at least a factor \(\min_c k/(d_c+k)\) times the rank-aware shared-span sum of the cover \(S\). When that sum is about \(M\) times one binomial, this is an additive \(\ln\max_c((d_c+k)/k)\) in \(\ln M\). It bounds what (1) can certify, not the true joint rank.

Nonvacuity. The increment ledger is met at \(\ell=16\) by a chain of \(19\) windows: block \(a\), \(1\le a\le19\), has as inputs the \(r_*\) consecutive bits \(x_a,\ldots,x_{a+r_*-1}\). Each span is a proper coordinate span of rank \(r_*>h(k+1)\); consecutive windows have \(e_a=1\) and sum without one. Two distinct windows meet in dimension at most \(r_*-1=h(k+1)\), so at this \(k\) no subspace of dimension above the threshold lies in two of them, and the shared-span count is \(C=19\). The saved check kernel_count_numbers.json gives \(k=302\), \(d=1034047\), \(k/(d+k)\approx2.92\cdot10^{-4}\), largest admissible chain length \(19\) with margin \(k^2-m\ln(4C_{\rm inc})\approx6.90\), and the published condition false already at \(M=2\). Degree \(D=107\) as in the shared-span entry. This witness shows that the hypotheses can hold; it is not a strict gain. At \(k'=k+1=303\) these windows are low-rank, so both the published and the shared-span counts exclude the family through the same degree.

Ceiling on the common-kernel step

Statement. In the same setting fix a degree \(D\ge2h+1\) and let \(K_D=\lfloor(n+1)/(2(D+1))\rfloor\), the largest \(k'\) with \(2k'(D+1)-1\le n\); assume \(K_D\ge1\). Put \(r_D=h(K_D+1)+1\), assume \(r_D+K_D<v\), and let \(q_D=\exp\bigl(-r_DK_D/(v-r_D-K_D)\bigr)\) and \(M_D=\lfloor K_D\ln(v+1)/q_D\rfloor+1\). There is a family of at most \(M_D\) distinct proper blocks, each with inputs \(r_D\) distinct old bits, such that for every \(k'\) with \(1\le k'\le K_D\), that is every \(k'\) the published theorem's degree condition \(2k'(D+1)-1\le n\) allows at this \(D\): every block has rank above \(h(k'+1)\), and no nonzero \(f\in\mathcal L_{k'}\) has zero ordinary restriction to all their flats. So at degree \(D\) the common-kernel step of the published proof, with any certificate of the joint rank, fails for this family at every \(k'\) its other conditions could allow. Here

\[\ln M_D\le\ln\bigl(K_D\ln(v+1)+1\bigr)+\frac{r_DK_D}{v-r_D-K_D},\qquad\frac{r_DK_D}{v-r_D-K_D}=\frac{3K_D^2}{m}\Bigl(1+O\Bigl(\frac1{K_D}+\frac{K_D}{m}\Bigr)\Bigr),\tag{3}\]

while the published theorem at degree \(D\) admits at most \(\ln(4M)\le K_D^2/m\), since it needs \(m\ln(4M)\le k'^2\) with \(k'\le K_D\).

Proof. For \(k'\le K_D\), \(h(k'+1)\le h(K_D+1)<r_D\) and \(\mathcal L_{k'}\subseteq\mathcal L_{K_D}\); it suffices to make the joint kernel in \(\mathcal L_{K_D}\) zero. For a set \(P\) of bits, ordinary restriction to \(x_P=0\) sends a row-linear monomial meeting \(P\) to zero and the others injectively to distinct monomials, so its kernel on \(\mathcal L_{K_D}\) is spanned by the row-linear monomials meeting \(P\); the joint kernel of several is spanned by those meeting every \(P_a\), since an intersection of spans of subsets of one basis is the span of their intersection. The constant meets nothing, so the joint kernel is zero exactly when no nonconstant row-linear monomial of degree at most \(K_D\), a set \(X\) of at most \(K_D\) bits, meets every \(P_a\). Choose \(P_1,\ldots,P_{M_D}\) independently and uniformly among the \(r_D\)-subsets of the \(v\) bits. For \(|X|=j\le K_D\),

\[\Pr[X\cap P=\varnothing]=\prod_{i=0}^{r_D-1}\Bigl(1-\frac{j}{v-i}\Bigr)\ge\Bigl(1-\frac{K_D}{v-r_D}\Bigr)^{r_D}\ge q_D,\tag{4}\]

using \(\ln(1-x)\ge-x/(1-x)\) at \(x=K_D/(v-r_D)\). So \(X\) meets all the sets with probability at most \(e^{-q_DM_D}\). The number of nonconstant row-linear monomials of degree at most \(K_D\) is below \(\dim\mathcal L_{K_D}\le\sum_jv^j/j!\le(v+1)^{K_D}\), and \(q_DM_D>K_D\ln(v+1)\), so by the union bound some choice has zero joint kernel. Repeated sets may be removed; inputs are linear forms, so each block is proper of rank \(r_D\). For the logarithm, \(M_D\le(K_D\ln(v+1)+1)/q_D\) since \(q_D\le1\). For the asymptotic form, \(r_DK_D=3\ell K_D^2(1+1/K_D+1/(3\ell K_D))\) and \(v-r_D-K_D=m\ell(1-O(K_D/m))\).

At \(\ell=16\). The saved run output kernel_count_numbers_run.txt gives, at \(D=107\): \(K_D=303\), \(r_D=14593\), \(\ln M_D\le12.62\) against the published \(K_D^2/m\approx1.40\); at \(D=97=2h+1\): \(K_D=334\), \(r_D=16081\), \(\ln M_D\le13.64\) against \(1.70\). The ratios, about \(9.0\) and \(8.0\), are dominated at this size by the logarithmic term; in a regime where \(K_D^2/m\) grows faster than \(\ln(K_D\ln v)\), such as the published polylogarithmic one, the ratio tends to \(3\).

Degenerate cases. In (1), a single covering subspace gives \(N=\binom{d_1+k}{k}\); \(e_c=0\) is the case \(T_{b(c)}\subseteq T_c\), handled by the cover \(S\) above; \(1\in T_c+T_{b(c)}\) gives the plain term. With no proper high-rank block the theorem takes \(f=1\). In the ceiling statement \(r_D+K_D<v\) keeps (4) positive, and \(K_D\ge1\) is needed for any \(k'\) to exist; the blocks are constructed, so the statement is not vacuous.

New mathematics. Braun's exclusion with the common-kernel count paid by span increments along any ordering of covering subspaces, containing the published and shared-span counts, together with the proof that this count is at least a factor \(\min_ck/(d_c+k)\) times the rank-aware sum of some cover. A first-moment family of coordinate blocks with no common row-linear kernel at any degree parameter allowed by a fixed degree \(D\), so that at degree \(D\) no certificate of the common-kernel step extends the admissible \(\ln M\) beyond \(3K_D^2/m\,(1+o(1))+\ln(K_D\ln(v+1)+1)\), against the published \(K_D^2/m\). Both proofs are elementary: the first composes lem:restriction-rank-overlap with the published ledger, the second is a union bound over row-linear monomials.

Selection gain. The kernel count can be paid per unit of span distance from a parent, but this gains at most a factor \(\max_c(d_c+k)/k\) over the rank-aware sum of some cover. At a fixed degree \(D\), any improvement of the joint-rank certificate in the common-kernel step reaches at most \(\ln M\approx3K_D^2/m+\ln(K_D\ln v)\) on unrestricted families, a factor tending to \(3\) over the published reach where \(K_D^2/m\) dominates, and about \(8\) to \(9\) at \(\ell=16\), where the logarithmic term dominates. Larger gains for Braun's adapter need structure of the family, such as shared subspaces, or a step other than the common kernel.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The increment count is a further structural sufficient condition whose gain over the rank-aware sum is at most a factor \(\max_c(d_c+k)/k\), an additive \(\ln((d+k)/k)\approx8.1\) in \(\ln M\) at \(\ell=16\), \(k=302\): larger than the published reach there. The ceiling proposition bounds one formulation, joint-rank savings within Braun's common-kernel step on unrestricted families at a fixed degree; it is not a refutation of joint-error-savings, which asks for a saving on some nontrivial unbounded family, and the families met on the goal's route are still not identified. Bearing. At \(\ell=16\) and \(D=107\), the ceiling ledger places a family of at most \(e^{12.62}\approx3\cdot10^5\) coordinate blocks beyond every certificate of the common-kernel step at every \(k'\le303\), where the published count reaches \(\ln(4M)\le1.40\); the increment ledger admits a 19-window chain at \(k=302\), which the published and shared-span counts also exclude at \(k'=303\). Not covered. A family that the increment count excludes and no recorded count does; arguments other than the common-kernel step; the actual error families of the goal's route; Pang's adapter.

Record search. Searches for random coordinate flats, hitting sets and transversals with no common kernel, limits of Braun's common-kernel method, and inventory necessity by first moment returned conj:replay-weight-barrier (a random-family barrier conjecture in a different \(\mathbb F_3\) setting), prop:duplicate-prefix-rank-law and unrelated restriction results; none states a family with no common row-linear kernel. The resemblance check returned prop:independent-affine-common-kernel, whose registered statement describes the common Boolean vanishing kernel of jointly independent affine groups by the monomials meeting every group; the ceiling proof uses the same description for ordinary restriction to coordinate flats, which need not be independent. The resemblance check also returned thm:bit-affine-all-ranks-dimension-criterion, whose registered statement excludes a degree-\(D\) one-level refutation when the sum of the high-flat restriction dimensions is below \(\dim\mathcal L_k\) and \(n\ge2k(D+1)-1\): it sums exact restriction dimensions where the increment limit above sums their binomial bounds, and the ceiling defeats it too, since it is a certificate of the common-kernel step. The preceding entry's searches cover the increment form. The numbers were computed by the committed script kernel_count_numbers.py, whose expectations and refuting outputs were committed before each run.

Verification gate. Dependencies read: the preceding entry's lemma with its increment form and the shared-span theorem with its proof, Braun's Section 5, and the definition of \(\mathcal L_k\) and the row-separation theorem's statement in Section 4. All were done. No Lean verification. Review record. Process audit. Nested spans do not contain the shared-subspace count; clique heads are per-block

16 October 2026 · Source comparison, no new claim · Shared-span relations. Produced by claude-code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning medium).

Where we stand. Unchanged: A source comparison; no living statement changes.

Chosen. This entry takes the sub-idea entry-2026-10-16-increment-count:s1 of the Mayer–Vietoris lead, in its third entry of the spell, rather than the head. It bears on joint-error-savings through the scheduled review that follows: whether shared-subspace clustering is already recorded decides how the review weighs the structured-family option.

Owed attempt. The preceding entry named the attempt: “read both statements and proofs and record the relation”. It was made for the nested-span theorem and its Lemma 8.2, statements and proofs. For the clique-head theorem only its registered statement was read; its proof and its base criterion were not, so that half of the attempt is unmade. It waits because that criterion and proof sit in another notebook's record and the route review, due next by the cadence, comes first; reading them is the next attempt on this item.

General statement. No new registered claim applies: this entry compares thm:shared-span-affine-exclusion with two recorded results whose hypotheses also restrict the family's structure.

Two comparisons

Nested spans. The historical Theorem 8.1, nested-span batch elimination, was read with its proof in the immutable handoff (chapter 8). Same-level blocks fresh for the old system with nested input spans \(V_1\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_s\) and inputs of degree at most \(\delta\) are eliminated at degree \(B=D+(p-1)\delta\), independent of the chain length. The proof learns each input \(f\) of the current block as a consequence: every later block's span contains \(f\), so a selector substitution weighted by \(\chi_\alpha(f)\) turns the original refutation into a derivation of \(\chi_\alpha(f)\), and the learned inputs are reused. It contains no common-kernel argument and no count over blocks. Its Lemma 8.2 relaxes nesting to nesting modulo a space \(H\), \((V_1+H)/H\subseteq\cdots\subseteq(V_s+H)/H\), provided every element of \(H\) has a supplied derivation from the old system within degree \(c\), at degree \(\max\{c,D+(p-1)\delta,(p+1)\delta\}\). Neither contains the shared-subspace count. The shared-span hypothesis allows incomparable spans: in the shared-span entry's family the spans \(T_1\oplus\Lambda\) of equal dimension are pairwise incomparable. Taking \(H=T_1\) in Lemma 8.2 leaves the parts \(\Lambda\) incomparable modulo \(T_1\), and a shared span of fresh-block inputs carries no supplied derivations from the old system. Conversely the nested results need no row-linear space or high-rank threshold. The two theorems are of different kinds, a reduction to the old system against an exclusion through degree \(D\) under conditions on \(m,k,D\); whether their settings coincide on a family where both might apply is not examined, and no comparison of their costs is claimed.

Clique heads. The registered statement of thm:allprime-clique-graph-rank-exclusion, an all-prime theorem in the odd-prime notebook, adds to the low-or-dense graph rank criterion of thm:allprime-low-or-dense-graph-rank-exclusion the blocks satisfying a clique-head hypothesis on a coordinate set: every \(\delta\)-subset of the set is a leading monomial of the block's span plus the projected old ideal. That hypothesis is on each block separately, and lowers the rank a block needs to join the criterion. Whether the criterion's count over blocks could be grouped by shared subspaces is not settled by the statement: the block count enters through the base criterion, whose hypotheses and proof were not read, and through a threshold its registered assessment records with a \(\log(8(M+1))\) term.

New mathematics. None: a comparison of statements and proofs with the recorded shared-span theorem.

Selection gain. Unchanged. The nested results do not contain shared-subspace clustering; the clique-head comparison is incomplete.

Goal progress. Unchanged. No implication of joint-error-savings changes. Bearing. Nothing at the goal's parameters: a comparison of recorded theorems with one of this line. It was spent because, had either result contained the shared-subspace count, the structured-family option before the review would have been a restatement of recorded work; for the nested results it is not.

Record search. The two results were found by the preceding entry's search for sliding windows, chain families and shared-span grouping. The nested theorem and Lemma 8.2 were read in the historical chapter, and the clique-head theorem by its registered statement. No further search was made in this entry.

Verification gate. The sole fresh-context medium reviewer of this cycle read the draft, the nested theorem with its proof and Lemma 8.2, and the registered statements. It passed the nested comparison's main point. It required Lemma 8.2 to be added, unsupported cost and setting comparisons to be removed, the clique-head theorem's all-prime scope to be stated, its block counting to be marked unread, and the closure to be Continuing, since the clique-head proof was not read. All were done. Review record. Process audit. The reviewer found the first title, “Nested spans and clique heads do not contain the shared-subspace count”, overstated for the clique-head half, and the title was rewritten after review.
